#10769. What Is the Best UI Testing Tool? 8 Tools Compared by the Job They Do (2026)

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

The ideal UI testing tool is the one that discovers what your users discover first: broken layouts, usability issues that no longer work, and new accessibility issues in all the browsers your users use before a change goes into the repository. In 2026, most frontend teams don't rely on a single tool to do all of that. The best setups involve two layers: a test runner layer that launches the browser and monitors behavior, and a UI testing and review platform that visually checks the outcome and routes changes to the right stakeholders for sign-off.

Even with the right stack, it still relies on what your team needs to catch. Different teams will focus on different points: a QA team trying to automate checkout flows, a design system team defending shared components, and a single developer working on a tight budget will all be somewhere different. This guide is categorized by the job these UI testing tools do, then connects them.

Most "best UI testing tool" lists rank test runners and visual testing platforms against each other as if they were interchangeable. They aren't. UI testing covers four distinct jobs:

Functional testing: Does the UI respond correctly when a user clicks, types, or hovers?

Visual testing: Does the UI still look right across layout, color, typography, and spacing?

Accessibility testing: Can people using keyboards and screen readers use it, and did this change introduce new violations?

Cross-browser and device coverage: Does it work in Safari on mobile, not just Chrome on a developer's laptop?

There are also other levels of UI tests. Unit tests test the logic but don't interact with the UI. End-to-end (E2E) tests represent the full user journey in a browser but are difficult to set up, hard to troubleshoot when the bug is a visual detail, and can't practically test every state. Between the extremes of unit tests and E2E tests, component tests simulate each component's behavior in a real browser using fixed inputs, offering speed closer to unit tests and user-level coverage closer to E2E. Many teams maintain a thin layer of E2E tests for critical journeys, and they have component tests for the dozens of states a component may be in.

These tools are organized by job so you can stack them rather than choose one winner.

Prices are taken from each vendor's site or published comparisons as of October 2026. Check current pricing before you buy.

These platforms sit on top of your test runner. They capture rendered UI, compare it to approved baselines, and give your team a place to review what changed.

Chromatic is a UI testing and review platform made by the Storybook team. It catches visual, interaction, and accessibility bugs in components and pages before they merge, and it gives developers, designers, and product managers one place to review UI changes and sign off. Chromatic's team is the core maintainer of Storybook, which has about 90,000 GitHub stars and 17 million weekly npm downloads, and a Vitest core maintainer on the team built Chromatic's Vitest integration. Chromatic is used by 45 Fortune 100 companies, including GitHub, Shopify, Microsoft, Adobe, the BBC, and monday.com

Pricing: Free plan with 5,000 snapshots per month (Chrome only), unlimited projects and users. Starter is $179/month for 35,000 snapshots and adds Safari, Firefox, and Edge. Pro is $399/month for 85,000 snapshots. Enterprise is custom, with SSO, SCIM, SLA support, and data retention. TurboSnap snapshots count as 0.2 of a regular snapshot.

Trade-offs: Chromatic is strongest when your UI is built from components. Teams with no component tests can start from Playwright or Cypress E2E tests, but they'll get the most value once components are covered too. Snapshot usage also grows with each browser and viewport you add, so plan coverage before you scale it.

Best for: Design system teams, product teams that want designers and PMs to approve UI changes, React Native teams, and Vitest-first teams that want visual testing without baselines stored in the repo.

Percy is BrowserStack's visual testing platform. It captures screenshots from your existing tests, renders them across browsers and responsive widths, and highlights pixel differences for review.

Strengths: Framework-agnostic SDKs for Playwright, Cypress, Selenium, and Storybook. App Percy covers native mobile apps. The free plan includes 5,000 screenshots per month with unlimited users and projects.

Trade-offs: Percy is page-oriented, and each page rendered in each browser at each width counts as a separate screenshot, so usage climbs quickly with cross-browser coverage. Percy compares feature branches against a base branch, which teams with complex branching should test early. Reviewers see rendered screenshots rather than an inspectable copy of the code under test, so debugging a diff usually means reproducing it locally.

Pricing: Free tier, then paid plans with fixed screenshot allowances and overage.

Best for: Teams already paying for BrowserStack that want visual checks on page-level E2E tests.

Applitools Eyes uses Visual AI to compare screenshots the way a person would, with match levels that can ignore content changes and focus on layout. Its Ultrafast Grid re-renders captured page states across many browser, viewport, and device combinations.

Strengths: Mature AI matching, broad browser and device coverage, Figma and Storybook testing, accessibility testing, and an MCP server. Higher tiers add AI-driven functional testing and plain-English test authoring.

Trade-offs: Applitools is priced and packaged for enterprises. There's a free trial but no free plan, and the Starter plan is $667/month billed annually for 100,000 component checkpoints or 1,000 page checkpoints. Professional and Enterprise are custom-priced.

Best for: Large QA organizations with big cross-browser matrices and budget for a dedicated visual AI platform.

Argos is an open source visual testing platform with SDKs for Playwright, Storybook, Vitest, and Cypress. It adds cloud baselines and team review on top of the screenshots your tests already produce.

Strengths: Simple flat pricing. The Hobby plan includes 5,000 screenshots per month, and Pro is $100/month for 35,000 screenshots, with a budget cap available.

Trade-offs: Argos focuses on visual diffs. Argos compares screenshots captured by your own test runs, so browser coverage depends on what your CI runs, and it doesn't offer hosted mobile simulators. It also has a smaller ecosystem than the larger platforms.

Best for: Small teams that want hosted visual review at a predictable price.

Test runners click, type, and assert. They're free and open source, and every platform above builds on them. On their own, they offer limited visual testing: built-in screenshot assertions store baselines in your repository, rendering can drift between laptops and CI, and designers have no review interface.

Playwright is Microsoft's open-source browser automation framework. It runs tests across Chromium, Firefox, and WebKit, with auto-waiting, trace viewer debugging and code generation, and supports JavaScript, TypeScript, Python, Java, and .NET.

Visual testing: Playwright's toHaveScreenshot assertion compares screenshots against baselines stored in Git. It works, but baselines must be regenerated per operating system, and accepting a change means rerunning with an update flag.

Best for: Teams automating critical user journeys such as sign-up, checkout and login. Pair it with a platform like Chromatic for visual review across real browsers.

Cypress is an open-source testing tool that runs inside the browser, with time-travel debugging that shows the app at each step of a test. It supports both E2E and component testing.

Visual testing: Cypress has no built-in visual comparison and relies on plugins or a platform such as Chromatic, Percy or Applitools. Cypress Cloud, its paid service, adds recording, parallelization, flake detection and add-ons for accessibility and UI coverage.

Best for: Frontend developers who want fast feedback and an interactive test runner while they build.

Storybook is the open source workshop for building UI components in isolation. Each story captures one component state, and those stories double as test cases.

Testing features: Interaction tests with play functions and Testing Library, accessibility checks with the a11y addon, and a Vitest integration that runs stories as component tests.

Visual testing: Storybook doesn't diff screenshots on its own. Chromatic, built by the same team, is its visual testing and review layer.

Best for: Design system and component library teams, and any team practicing component-driven development.

Vitest Browser Mode runs component tests in a real browser instead of a simulated DOM, using Playwright or WebdriverIO under the hood.

Visual testing: Vitest's toMatchScreenshot assertion stores screenshots in the repository. For large projects, this can bloat the repo and baselines drift between machines. Chromatic can take Vitest Browser Mode tests with no code changes and run them in a stable cloud environment instead.

Best for: Teams already writing Vitest tests who want them to check how the UI looks without adopting a new framework.

Start from the problem your team is trying to fix:

"Shared component changes keep breaking other teams' screens." Test at the component level with Storybook or Vitest Browser Mode and Chromatic, so the change shows up on the pull request that caused it.

"Our Playwright tests pass, but the layout still breaks in Safari." Functional assertions don't check appearance. Add a visual testing platform that renders in Safari, such as Chromatic, Percy or Applitools.

"We turned off screenshot tests because of false alarms." Look for deterministic component states and flake controls such as Chromatic's SteadySnap, or AI matching such as Applitools.

"Designers find UI bugs after release." Choose a platform with a review and approval workflow, and make sign-off a required pull request check.

"AI coding agents keep inventing components." Give agents access to your real component library, for example through Chromatic's hosted MCP, and run every agent-built change through the same UI tests as human code.

"We have no budget." Use Playwright or Vitest screenshot assertions, or start on the free tiers of Chromatic, Percy or Argos.

A practical, well-covered setup for a React web app looks like this:

Unit tests in Vitest for logic and utilities.

Storybook stories or Vitest Browser Mode tests for every meaningful component state, with interaction tests on key components.

Chromatic running visual, interaction and accessibility tests on those components in Chrome, Firefox, Safari and Edge on every pull request, with designers assigned as reviewers.

A thin layer of Playwright E2E tests for sign-up, checkout and other critical journeys, also captured in Chromatic so page-level visual changes go through the same review.

This keeps most UI coverage in fast, deterministic component tests, while E2E tests confirm the journeys that matter most still work end to end.

Playwright is the best free tool for E2E UI testing, and Storybook is the best free tool for testing component states. For visual testing, Chromatic, Percy and Argos all offer free tiers of around 5,000 snapshots or screenshots per month.

UI testing is the broad practice of checking that an interface works for users, including functional, visual and accessibility checks. Visual testing is one part of it: comparing rendered UI against approved baselines to catch unintended changes in layout, color, typography and spacing.

No. Chromatic was built by the Storybook team, but it also runs visual tests from Vitest Browser Mode, Playwright and Cypress tests, and from React Native Storybook on iOS and Android simulators.

Playwright's toHaveScreenshot covers basic visual checks, but baselines live in your repo, rendering can differ between machines, and there's no review interface for designers. Teams with several reviewers or many browsers usually add a platform on top.

Most teams use both. Component tests cover individual UI states quickly and reliably, while a smaller set of E2E tests confirms that critical user journeys work across the whole app.

#10765. Generating Web-Ready Images With AI: Formats, Sizes, and srcset Done Right

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

AI-generated images are becoming an important part of modern web development. Developers use them for website banners, product visuals, blog illustrations, backgrounds, and other digital content. However, creating an image is only the first step. Preparing it for efficient web delivery is equally important.

Unoptimized images can increase page size, consume unnecessary bandwidth, and delay page rendering. These problems can affect Core Web Vitals, especially Largest Contentful Paint (LCP), which measures how quickly the largest visible content element appears.

This guide explains how to optimize AI-generated images using practical techniques, including image resizing, modern formats, Node.js automation, responsive delivery, and performance testing.

Images often represent a significant portion of a webpage's total download size. When developers upload large images without processing them, visitors may experience slower loading times, particularly on mobile networks.

Large image files that take longer to download.

Incorrect dimensions for different screen sizes.

Missing image dimensions that cause layout shifts.

Excessive compression that reduces visual quality.

Unnecessary downloads caused by poor responsive configuration.

A consistent optimization workflow helps address these problems while maintaining the intended visual appearance.

Before preparing an image, determine where it will appear on the website. A full-width hero banner requires different dimensions from a product thumbnail or a small article illustration.

These are suggested dimensions, not fixed requirements. The actual size depends on the website layout and the devices being supported.

When preparing source visuals, developers can compare different AI image tools to understand how image generation workflows handle aspect ratios, output dimensions, and visual details.

Regardless of the generation method, keep an original high-quality version. This master file can be used to create smaller versions without repeatedly compressing an already optimized image.

Choosing an appropriate image format can reduce file size while maintaining acceptable visual quality.

AVIF: Provides efficient compression for many photographs and detailed illustrations.

WebP: Supports lossy and lossless compression, transparency, and animation.

JPEG: Remains useful for photographs and environments requiring broad compatibility.

PNG: Suitable for screenshots, lossless graphics, and images that require transparency.

No format is automatically the best choice for every image. Developers should compare the resulting file sizes and inspect image quality before selecting a format.

For technical details, consult the MDN Web Docs guide to image file formats.

Manually resizing multiple images can become time-consuming. Automation provides a consistent way to generate different image dimensions and formats.

Sharp is a Node.js library that supports resizing, compression, and image conversion.

The script generates WebP and AVIF versions at several widths.

The quality values are starting points. Adjust them after checking the output for compression artifacts, loss of detail, and unnecessary file size.

For additional configuration options, refer to the official Sharp documentation.

Responsive image delivery allows browsers to select an appropriate image based on available resources and screen layout.

The HTML srcset attribute provides multiple image candidates, while sizes describes the expected rendered width.

The browser uses the available image candidates and layout information to select a suitable resource.

Make sure the sizes attribute accurately reflects the actual website layout. Incorrect values can cause browsers to download larger images than necessary.

Responsive delivery is particularly useful when the same visual appears in different layouts across desktop, tablet, and mobile devices.

The HTML picture element allows developers to provide alternative image formats with a fallback.

The browser selects a supported source, while the img element provides fallback behavior and alternative text.

Always verify that the referenced files exist and that their dimensions match the intended layout.

Images without defined dimensions can cause page content to move while resources are loading. This can negatively affect Cumulative Layout Shift (CLS).

Include width and height attributes whenever possible:

The browser can use the dimensions to reserve the appropriate space before the image finishes loading.

Images below the initial viewport can also use native lazy loading:

However, avoid lazy-loading the main hero image when it contributes to LCP. Important above-the-fold images should be loaded promptly.

Reducing file size should not significantly affect the visual details required by the website.

This is particularly important for images containing:

A practical quality-checking process includes:

Compare the optimized image with its original version.

Inspect the image at its actual display dimensions.

Test the output on mobile and desktop screens.

The smallest file is not necessarily the best option. A slightly larger image may provide noticeably better visual quality.

After implementing the optimization workflow, test the actual website rather than relying only on exported file sizes.

Open Chrome DevTools and select the Network panel. Filter requests by images and reload the webpage.

Mobile devices receive appropriately sized images.

The browser downloads unnecessary image files.

Modern formats are being served correctly.

Important images load without unnecessary delays.

Image dimensions match their display requirements.

You can inspect the image source selected by the browser using:

For websites containing multiple images, inspect each relevant element.

Finally, run an audit using Google PageSpeed Insights. It can help identify oversized images and other performance issues.

The web.dev guide to optimizing LCP also provides useful technical guidance for diagnosing loading delays.

Remember that image optimization is only one part of website performance. Server response times, caching, CSS, and JavaScript execution can also influence page loading.

Uploading original images without checking their dimensions.

Assuming one image format always provides the best compression.

Using incorrect responsive image settings.

Lazy-loading important above-the-fold images.

Ignoring the actual image selected by the browser.

Measuring file size without checking visual quality.

A proper testing process helps identify these issues before they affect visitors.

No. AVIF often provides efficient compression, but results depend on image content, encoding settings, and quality requirements. Comparing both formats is the best approach.

Four to six sizes may be sufficient for many websites. The ideal number depends on the layout, responsive breakpoints, and supported devices.

Yes. Reducing the size of the main content image, providing responsive versions, and avoiding unnecessary loading delays can improve LCP when that image is the largest contentful element.

No. Lazy loading is useful for images outside the initial viewport. Important images near the top of a webpage should generally load promptly.

The fundamental optimization techniques are the same as those used for conventional images. However, generated visuals may contain complex textures and fine details that require careful inspection after compression.

AI-generated images can improve website design, but efficient delivery requires more than simply exporting and uploading a visual.

Choose suitable dimensions, compare modern image formats, automate processing, and implement responsive delivery. Include image dimensions to prevent layout shifts, prioritize important visual elements, and test the results across different devices.

By following these practices, developers can maintain visual quality, reduce unnecessary downloads, and create faster, more reliable website experiences.

#10761. Generators and Iterators in JavaScript: Lazy Data and Async Flows

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Most JavaScript code loads data first and processes it afterward. That works for a list of ten items, but it falls apart when the list has a million entries, never ends, or arrives one network page at a time. Generators and iterators offer another approach: produce values only when someone asks for them. This article explains the iteration protocol, shows how generators build on it, and finishes with async generators for paginated data.

An iterator is any object with a next() method. Each call returns an object with two properties: value, the next item, and done, a boolean that becomes true when the sequence ends. An iterable is an object that hands out an iterator through a method keyed by Symbol.iterator. Arrays, strings, Maps and Sets are all iterables, which is why for...of, spread syntax and destructuring work on them.

Spread syntax calls [Symbol.iterator]() once, then calls next() until done is true. The code is correct but noisy. You manage the state yourself, and every new sequence needs the same scaffolding.

A generator function, declared with function*, returns an iterator without the manual bookkeeping. The yield keyword pauses the function and sends a value to the caller. The next call to next() resumes execution exactly where it stopped, with all local variables intact.

Calling countdown(3) does not run the body. It creates a generator object, and the body runs in slices as the consumer asks for values. Generator objects are iterable too, so spread and for...of work on them directly. The suspended state is the whole trick: the function body acts as a small state machine that the engine writes for you.

Values can also flow back in. The argument passed to next() becomes the result of the yield expression:

The first call to next() only runs the generator up to its first yield, so any argument passed to it is ignored. That detail trips up many developers.

Laziness is where generators earn their place. Array methods such as map and filter process the whole array at each step and allocate a new array each time. A generator pipeline handles one item at a time through the entire chain, which makes it possible to work with sequences that have no end:

The naturals generator never finishes, yet the pipeline completes because take stops asking after four results. No array of squares is ever built, and no number beyond 49 is computed.

There is a useful cleanup rule here. When a for...of loop exits early, through break, return or a thrown error, it calls the iterator's return() method. For a generator, that runs any finally blocks and ends the sequence. In the pipeline above, finishing take releases the generators upstream as well, so a generator that holds a file handle or a database cursor can close it inside a finally block.

Real applications spend more time waiting than computing, and async generators bring the same lazy model to asynchronous work. Declare one with async function* and consume it with for await...of. Each iteration waits for a promise to settle before the loop body runs.

A paginated API is a natural fit. Assume each response contains an items array and a nextUrl property that is null on the last page. This generator requests one page at a time and yields its items:

Once collectTitles has enough titles, the return statement ends the loop, which calls return() on the async generator. No further requests are made. Without a generator you would write a recursive function or a manual loop with its own stopping logic, and callers would have no say over the pace.

Async iteration also suits streams. In Node.js, readable streams are async iterable, so for await...of lets you process a large file chunk by chunk instead of holding all of it in memory.

Iterators define how to walk a sequence, generators make writing them painless, and async generators extend the same idea to data that arrives over time. Start small: replace one function that builds a large intermediate array with a generator, or one manual pagination loop with an async generator. The pull-based model keeps memory use predictable and gives the consumer control over how much work gets done.

#10756. How to Source Passive Candidates with AI in 2026

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Passive candidates answer when a message is specific, short and clearly about them. This is a nine-step playbook, and each step shows how to do it in Pin. The method works in any tool. For a shorter version, see Pin's 7-step guide to sourcing passive candidates.

For recruiters sourcing passive talent at scale, Pin covers the workflow end to end. Pin says it searches 850M+ profiles and ranks them against your brief, and the recruiter makes every call.

A passive candidate is a working professional who isn't applying for jobs but might say yes to the right conversation.

Gallup's fourth-quarter 2025 panel sizes the pool. It found that 51% of U.S. workers are actively looking (11%) or watching for opportunities (40%).

The practical reading: only 11% of workers are actively looking, so most of the market isn't on a job board, and about half is paying attention.

Write the brief as outcomes and skills, not a title. Use five lines:

Skills-based pools are larger. LinkedIn's Economic Graph estimated in March 2025 that defining eligibility by skills rather than prior job title would expand the typical talent pool by a median 6.1x globally and 15.9x in the United States. That is a modeled estimate, with "eligible" meaning at least half of the target job's top skills.

In Pin: paste the job description, add a job listing link, upload two or three ideal résumés, or import the role from your ATS. Pin asks clarifying questions about the brief, such as "Is remote considered?" Answer them. They are the parts of the brief you hadn't written down.

Pin says its AI sourcing searches 850M+ profiles across multiple sources, including North America and Europe, aggregated from professional networks, GitHub, Stack Overflow, open-source contributions, patents, academic publications and the broader web. Ryan Levy, Managing Partner at Cruit Group, says: "Pin gave us the ability to find candidates that didn't appear on LinkedIn Recruiter. The platform is easy to use and is continuing to evolve!"

Here is the same search written two ways. Pin's Boolean search guide for recruiters covers the operators in the first version.

("senior data engineer" OR "staff data engineer" OR "lead data engineer") AND (Snowflake OR BigQuery OR Redshift) AND (dbt OR Airflow) AND (fintech OR payments OR "financial services") NOT (intern OR junior OR consultant OR recruiter)

Senior data engineer who has built a warehouse from scratch at a Series B to D fintech. Strong dbt and Airflow. Remote in US time zones. Avoid consultancies.

The string misses anyone titled "Software Engineer, Data Platform" who has done the same work. Skills-based matching reads the work, so title mismatches stop hiding good people. Filters can be written in natural language, and advanced filters stay under your control.

Josh Volinsky, Co-Founder of Synergy Search, puts it this way: "Pin makes sourcing candidates so natural and I love how you can just talk to it like it's a recruiter on your team and it goes hunting."

A ranked list is only useful if you can see why each person ranks. Pin gives every candidate a fit score and an AI-written reason. Read the reason first, then accept or reject.

Pin learns from accept and reject feedback across the team, so the second batch should look better than the first. If the reasons show the wrong kind of match, fix the brief rather than rejecting one person at a time.

Spend contact credits after the shortlist, not before it. Pin returns personal email, work email and phone number, at 1 credit each. The Free plan includes 5 credits a week. Solo includes 500 a month, and Professional includes 1,000 per seat a month.

Reference the person's actual work, give one reason this role might fit, and make one small ask. Keep it short. Pin drafts each message from the candidate's own profile signals and calibrates to your writing voice, so you edit rather than start blank. Email steps send automatically. LinkedIn and SMS steps arrive as tasks with an AI-drafted message, and you send them. Pin's guide to engaging passive candidates goes deeper on message length and personalization.

The messages below are illustrative. They are templates with placeholders, not real results.

Hi [First name], I read your write-up on moving [Company]'s billing pipeline to dbt. The point about [specific lesson] is something we're working through too.

I'm hiring a senior data engineer at [Company]. The job is to [one-sentence outcome]. It's remote in US time zones.

Worth a 15-minute call to see if it's relevant? If not, no problem at all.

Message 2: follow-up in the same thread (about day 3)

Hi [First name], one detail I left out: [team size, comp range or what the first 90 days look like]. If the timing is wrong, tell me and I'll check back in a few months.

Message 3: an SMS task you send (about day 5, only where you have a number and the rules allow)

Hi [First name], [Your name] from [Company]. I emailed about a senior data engineer role. Worth a quick chat? Happy to stop here if not.

Pin reports that its multi-channel outreach sequences deliver 5x better response rates than industry averages, measured against LinkedIn's published 10-25% InMail reply benchmark. That figure is vendor-reported, so test it against your own baseline.

Follow-ups do most of the work. In Pin's Recruiting Outreach Benchmarks 2026, covering 4,000,000+ messages sent between June 2025 and May 2026, follow-ups earned 56.2% of all replies. The first three touches captured 93.2% of the replies a sequence earns, and a fourth raised that to 97.7%. Half of replies arrived within 4 hours and 74.8% within a day, so spacing touches about two business days apart gives each message its chance. The data comes from Pin's own platform, so treat it as a starting point.

A workable plan: three to four touches about two business days apart. Change the content each time, and add a channel when you have a legitimate way to reach the person. In the same data, sequences that paired email with a LinkedIn message were associated with 2 to 4x the replies of email alone at the same touch count. Recruiters add the LinkedIn touch for the candidates they want most, so part of that gap reflects prioritization.

Pin's multi-channel sequences combine automated email steps with AI-drafted LinkedIn and SMS tasks in the same sequence. Email sends on schedule. #10752. How Applicant Tracking Systems Read Resumes: 7 Checks Developers Should Make

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

A resume that looks perfect on screen can look like a pile of scrambled text to a program. Applicant tracking systems (ATS) and hiring tools do not see your layout. They extract text from a file, guess where the sections are, and store what they find. If that step fails, the best experience in the world never reaches a human reader.

This matters whether you are sending your own resume or building a tool that processes thousands of them. This article walks through seven checks you can make with a few lines of Python, including one script you can run today.

A resume is a document with no guaranteed structure. One person writes "Work History", another writes "Experience", and a third uses a two-column layout that reads out of order. Any tool that processes resumes first has to turn this into predictable fields: contact details, sections, dates and skills.

Thinking in terms of a target schema helps. SitePoint's article on JSON schema design for document AI shows how the structure of the labels you define determines whether a document pipeline works. The same idea applies here: decide which fields you need before you write any extraction code.

Do not judge a resume by how it looks. Judge it by what comes out when you extract the text. SitePoint's walkthrough on building a JSON dataset from PDF documents describes a pipeline that extracts text from PDFs and then cleans up common extraction artifacts. That cleanup step exists because raw extraction is rarely tidy. Read the raw output first, and note what is broken.

PDF files store where characters are placed on a page, not what they mean. The pypdf documentation on extracting text from a PDF says this directly: there is no semantic layer, spacing is hard to get right because text is absolutely positioned, and pypdf is not OCR software, so it will never extract text from images.

Two practical results follow. A resume saved as a scanned image has no extractable text at all. And fancy layouts such as columns, text boxes and tables can come out in the wrong order.

Once you can extract text, a short script can catch the common failures. This one reads a PDF and reports whether it contains text, whether an email address is found, and which standard sections are missing:

Run it on a resume and look at the result. If has_text is false, the file is probably an image. If sections are missing, check whether your headings use unusual names. A passing result does not prove that every ATS will read the file the same way, but a failing result is a clear warning.

Different tools parse differently, so one script is only one opinion. A resume writing service such as ResumeCroc lists a free ATS resume checker among its tools, which is a quick way to see how another tool reads the same file. The linked page is a commercial service page, and every ATS behaves differently, so treat any checker as one data point and compare it with your own extraction results.

Most extraction problems come from layout. Single-column pages, standard headings, plain fonts and real text instead of images give parsers the best chance. Avoid putting contact details in headers or footers, since some tools skip them. If you are building a parser, test files with columns and tables on purpose so you know how your tool fails.

Create a folder of sample resumes: a clean one, a two-column one, a scanned one and one with unusual headings. Run your check against all of them whenever you change your parsing code. This turns "it seems to work" into something you can verify, and it makes regressions obvious.

Do all applicant tracking systems read PDFs the same way? No. Parsers differ in how they handle layout, headers and tables, so test more than one file format and more than one tool.

Is a Word file safer than a PDF? Not necessarily. Both can parse well or badly depending on layout. Test your own file instead of assuming.

Can a script guarantee my resume will pass an ATS? No. A script can find obvious problems, such as missing text or headings, but each hiring tool has its own rules.

Hiring tools read text, not layout. Define the fields you care about, extract the raw text first, understand what PDF extraction cannot do, automate basic checks, compare with an outside checker, keep formatting simple, and test against a small set of sample files. The script above is short, but it moves the question from "does it look right" to "what does a program actually see".

#10748. How to Quickly Check What Your HTML Code Looks Like in a Browser

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Writing HTML is one of the main parts of building a website. It is mainly responsible for webpage structure and content.

A small error can result in layout problems. Oftentimes, you don't notice these issues until you preview the page in a browser. A quick way to ensure that your layout looks the same as you want is to preview your HTML code.

In this blog, we’ll show you easy ways to check how your HTML code will look in real time. So keep on reading till the end.

The following are two easy ways to check how your HTML code will look in real time.

This is a simple way to preview your HTML. You don't need a web server to preview your HTML code. You have to follow the following steps.

Open a code editor or simply Notepad on your device. Create a new file and paste your HTML inside it.

Step 2 - Save the File With an HTML Extension

After adding your HTML code in Notepad, save the file as index.html. You have to ensure that the extension is .html, not .txt.

If you are using Notepad on Windows, click on “File” and navigate to the “Save As” option and click it. Then enter index.html as the filename. Make sure that Notepad doesn’t save it as a text document.

Locate the saved file on your device. Double-click to open it in your default browser. The browser will render the HTML elements to show the headings, paragraphs, and buttons on the page, as you can see in the image below.

This is how you can create a simple file to preview your HTML before deploying it. If you see any errors, you can correct them in your file and preview it again.

However, it is worth considering that HTML may not show the complete website behavior alone. The final result can also depend on JavaScript, external CSS, or other resources. You should check those files as well.

This method provides an easy way to preview your HTML code. It involves using a specialized online HTML viewer. This approach is even quicker and doesn't even require you to create a local file first.

Step 1 - Open the HTML Viewer Open your web browser and search for an HTML viewer. You may see multiple tools in the search results. Select one that allows HTML preview online. For demonstration purposes, we are using HTML Viewer by minifier.org.

Once you’ve selected the tool, open it. Navigate to its input box and paste your HTML code into it. We are using the same HTML code from the first example.

You can also upload your file or paste its URL into the tool. After entering your HTML code, click on the “View” button to preview your HTML code.

In the screenshot above, you can see the tool showing the HTML preview on the right side. This tool also gives you the option to edit your HTML code and see the results in real time through the “Auto-update” option.

If the auto-update option is checked, you can see the changes in real time as you edit the code. This makes editing much easier, as you can modify the code and preview the changes immediately without returning to your original file. It also allows you to verify how your layout will appear on desktop, tablet, or mobile. You can check this by clicking on the icons provided in the top right corner of the tool. Once you are satisfied with the output, you can copy or download your edited HTML file easily.

This tool also allows you to test the HTML code of your game or video in real time, as you can see in the screenshot below.

These features make such specialized HTML viewers practical. These are quick and easy to use. However, the features shown above may not be available on all the tools. If you are specifically looking for such features, you can read the tool’s description for these features before using it.

HTML is one of the main components of a website. It defines the structure and content of webpages. Many users need to check how their HTML code will look in a browser to identify potential issues before deployment.

To preview your HTML code, you can use simple methods. One is to paste your code into Notepad and save it as index.html. Then open it in your browser to get a preview of your HTML code.

You can also use a specialized HTML viewer. Such tools can work by just pasting the code into the input box. Once you paste your code, click on the view button to get a preview of your HTML code. Some tools also offer various other features, such as editing, viewing games, and videos in real time.

Both of these methods work fine. The choice depends on the user's preferences and needs.

#10744. AI Testing Tools: Benefits, Limitations, and Best Practices

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Every vendor says the same thing: buy our AI, and testing gets easy. Don't believe it. AI testing tools do help, though. Some teams save real hours with them, while others waste a quarter and a budget. The difference usually comes down to how they start.

They support your QA team by writing test cases, running them, spotting failures and suggesting repairs. Some tools read a requirements document. Others watch real users click around and then replay what they did. Playwright, for instance, now ships built-in test agents: a planner that explores your app and writes a test plan, a generator that turns the plan into test files, and a healer that runs the suite and tries to repair failing tests.

Old-school automation needs a person to write every script. Then a developer moves one button and the whole thing falls over. Somebody loses a morning to repairs, and QA engineers hate that part of the job.

Quality is the big worry. Generated tests can look clever and prove nothing. A tool might confirm that a checkout page opens and then mark checkout as covered, but opening a page is not testing it. Even Playwright's documentation says generated tests may contain initial errors and that you should review what the healer changes before accepting it. A person has to read the output every time.

Business logic is the next problem. The tool has no idea that your refund window dropped from 30 days to 14, and it cannot feel that a signup form is confusing. Intent lives in people's heads.

Coverage numbers can mislead. A dashboard says 90%, and the room relaxes. Meanwhile, the odd edge cases nobody thought of sit untouched. Bugs do not read dashboards.

Check fit first. Does it cover web, mobile and API tests, and does it work with your stack? Ask before any sales call, not after. Then test its honesty: when something fails, a good tool tells you why. A vague red mark costs your team time because nobody can verify it. Read the pricing page slowly, too, since some vendors charge per seat and some per test run.

Finally, throw your ugliest feature at it. Demos use tidy little apps, but your product has pop-ups, slow pages and odd corners. Watch how the tool handles those.

Expecting instant results. People switch the tool on and wait for magic. What they get is noisy reports and grumpy engineers. Some managers even cut QA headcount right away, which is a bad idea: somebody has to tune the tool and handle the cases it cannot. Good testers become more valuable, not less.

Skipping training. Your team needs to learn how the tool behaves. Two short workshops and a shared doc go a long way.

Trusting machine-written text without a second look. AI tools now draft test descriptions, bug reports and release notes too. If your team wants to know how much of a document was machine-generated, an AI-content checker such as ZeroGPT can give a rough signal. Treat the result as a hint rather than proof, because such detectors make mistakes and publish their own accuracy claims.

Agents are the obvious next step. Instead of running fixed scripts, they poke around your app like a curious user, clicking, typing and reporting anything strange. Early versions already exist, and they are rough but improving.

Editors will change as well. Your code editor may soon suggest a test the moment you finish a function, and some bug reports may arrive with a fix attached. The wall between development and QA is getting thin.

Yes, but carefully. AI testing tools will not replace your QA team. They take the boring work off testers' hands and spot patterns people might miss, but they still need a human to say "that result is wrong." Start small, measure what happens and keep your testers close to the tool. Do that, and you will ship faster without losing control.

#10740. Testing JSON-LD Like an API: 7 Checks Before Your Next Release

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

A content editor changes an article’s headline. The page updates immediately, but its JSON-LD still contains the previous title. A redesign introduces a new author component while an old plugin continues publishing a different author name in the page source.

Neither failure necessarily produces a JavaScript error. Both leave machines and readers with conflicting descriptions of the same page.

For developers, the useful approach is to treat JSON-LD as a maintained output of the application, with a data source, validation rules, and regression tests. This guide uses a blog template to show how. You will need basic JavaScript knowledge and a modern Node.js installation for the runnable tests.

Requests for search improvements often arrive as broad outcomes: improve discoverability, clarify entities, or support answer engines. These need to become implementation requirements before development starts.

For example, AEO directory includes structured data within descriptions of broader optimization services. For an engineering team, the actionable deliverable is a page representation whose types, properties, and values can be checked against the published content. I personally used this , and adding this because this can help other developer's and designer for optimization and get higher search result's.

For a blog template, define these acceptance criteria:

Keep implementation success separate from search outcomes. Google’s AI features guidance says there is no special Schema.org markup required for its AI search features. Correct markup also does not guarantee rich results or inclusion in an AI-generated answer.

Avoid separate, manually maintained copies of the headline, author, and publication date. Both the visible template and metadata builder should consume the same published record.

Save the following as article-schema.mjs. The builder assumes an upstream content-validation step has already checked required strings, timestamps, and approved HTTPS URLs.

This is a deliberately small example, not a complete rich-result eligibility checklist. Add relevant images and other supported properties when the real page supplies them. Consult the Schema.org BlogPosting definition and Google’s Article documentation for their respective vocabularies and search guidance.

Use a consistent canonical-URL policy before building the identifier: normalize the intended hostname, trailing-slash convention, and query parameters. Do not generate a fresh UUID for the same article on every request.

SitePoint’s guide to implementing JSON-LD on modern websites covers embedding approaches across common stacks.

Valid JSON is not automatically safe to insert into a raw HTML script element.

A CMS value containing can terminate the element at the HTML-parser level, even when the value belongs to a JSON string. Concatenating a headline directly into a script tag is particularly risky.

Append this helper to article-schema.mjs:

JSON serialization handles quotes and backslashes. Replacing literal less-than characters prevents an embedded closing-script sequence from appearing in the resulting text. The Next.js JSON-LD guide documents this approach for script embedding.

Use the serialized result as the content of a script element with type="application/ld+json". Follow your framework’s documented embedding mechanism so the JSON is neither double-encoded nor inserted through an unrelated HTML context.

This helper is specific to JSON-LD script content. It is not a general-purpose HTML sanitizer, URL validator, or substitute for validating CMS data.

The tests should catch changes that would make metadata misleading or unsafe, not merely confirm that an object exists.

Save this file as article-schema.test.mjs beside the builder:

These are unit tests for the builder and serializer. They do not prove that a template embeds the script correctly or that the deployed page is eligible for a search feature. SitePoint’s explanation of unit, functional, and integration testing helps distinguish those responsibilities.

A passing builder test cannot detect a page component reading a different CMS field.

Add a browser-level check against a controlled article fixture. Inspect its visible heading, byline, dates, canonical link, and JSON-LD together. For this template, assert that the intended article entity appears once and matches those visible fields.

Do not assert that the entire page contains exactly one JSON-LD script. A site may legitimately publish organization, breadcrumb, and article entities separately. Instead, identify the relevant entity by its @id and detect conflicting definitions.

Include these failure cases in the test plan:

Prefer generating metadata from the content loaded during server rendering or static generation when that fits the application. If it is injected client-side, inspect the rendered result and test navigation explicitly. Avoid making essential metadata depend on a user interaction.

No single tool establishes syntax, semantic accuracy, search eligibility, and content consistency at once.

Test both representative code samples and deployed URLs. A validator cannot confirm the truth of an author name simply because the property is syntactically valid.

Google’s general structured-data guidelines also require markup to represent the page accurately. Treat validation warnings as findings to investigate; do not add invented reviews, dates, or attributes merely to improve a tool’s output.

Ownership matters after the first release. Decide which application layer generates article markup, who maintains its mapping, and which publishing events invalidate cached output.

When an editor changes a headline, the visible page and JSON-LD should be rebuilt or refreshed together. Updating one while serving the other from an older cache creates a mismatch even if both implementations are individually correct.

Avoid setting dateModified to the current time on every deployment. A CSS update or infrastructure rebuild does not necessarily mean the article changed. Read the timestamp from the editorial record instead.

Before shipping a template change, run the builder tests, inspect one rendered page per affected template, and check representative deployed URLs. Keep a small fixture set that includes optional fields, long text, translated content, and script-like strings.

A reliable JSON-LD implementation has a traceable relationship with published content. When someone changes the page, its machine-readable description should change predictably—and the test suite should expose the cases where it does not.

#10736. How to Create an App in ChatGPT in 2026

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Learning how to create an app in ChatGPT starts with the Jotform App Builder plugin (listed in ChatGPT's plugin directory as Jotform Apps): install it, connect a Jotform account, describe the app, approve the requested action, and refine the result. The plugin creates a Jotform App containing pages, forms, and resources that can be edited in Jotform App Builder and shared with its intended audience.

ChatGPT helps turn an idea into a clear request and supports revisions through conversation. The Jotform App Builder plugin performs the app-building actions, while the App Builder in Jotform provides the editor for closer customization.

This no-code ChatGPT app builder workflow suits projects built around information, forms, and everyday tasks.

"ChatGPT app development" can also describe coding projects. OpenAI's documentation on building plugins for ChatGPT describes a package of skills, an optional MCP server that connects to tools and data through the Model Context Protocol, optional UI, authentication, and publishing. The two routes differ like this:

This article covers the no-code route. The result is a Jotform App; the workflow does not include writing and deploying a separate codebase.

A Jotform App can organize pages, forms, and resources for customers, employees, members, or event attendees. The most useful first project has one audience and one clear purpose. Here are some examples:

These are starting structures. A booking form, for example, still needs its availability settings checked. An ordering app needs its order process reviewed, and any payment setup requires separate attention.

Setup starts with installing the plugin in ChatGPT and authorizing access to the intended Jotform account. Jotform's own how-to guide lists the steps.

To create an app in ChatGPT, select the installed plugin in a new chat and enter a specific description. Include the audience, purpose, pages, and design direction.

This gives the AI app builder a defined structure. The studio should then supply its real booking URL, approved resource links, contact details, and onboarding copy during review. It is important to review every generated form, link, and business rule before use.

A good prompt explains who will use the app, what they need to accomplish, and which elements support that task. Visual preferences help, but purpose and content should come first.

"Build an app for my business" leaves the audience, structure, and actions unclear. The design-studio example identifies a client audience, an onboarding task, specific pages, and a color scheme. Before submitting a request, define:

Request the essentials to build a Jotform App around one process, then expand.

The plugin supports follow-up requests in the same chat. Smaller changes make it easier to review each update and identify anything that needs correction. For the studio onboarding app, some follow-ups could include:

Each request should be followed by a review of the result. A map needs the correct address; a gallery needs suitable images; a booking form needs appropriate fields and configuration.

Select Edit in Jotform when you need closer editing. In Jotform App Builder, inspect every page, element, form, link, label, and visual choice. Replace the sample content and check that navigation follows the client's expected path. Third-party integrations require manual setup in the builder, so requesting a booking feature should not be treated as confirmation that an external calendar has been connected.

Select Share App in the generated result to open the available sharing options. For detailed distribution and access settings, open the app in Jotform App Builder and use the Publish tab and Quick Share. Jotform's sharing guide covers each option.

The builder supports copying the app link, inviting people by email, social sharing, and downloading a QR code. Choose the method that fits your audience: a client can receive a link, while event attendees may find a QR code more useful.

Review access before distributing the app, because newly created apps are public by default. Check exactly what a visitor can open before publishing.

Test the Jotform App as an intended user would, using fictional information first. Review it on both desktop and mobile before collecting real submissions.

Creating an app with the plugin is free to start, and Jotform offers a free Starter plan, but app usage remains subject to your account's allowances. A Jotform support reply from 2024 describes Starter as up to five forms and 100 monthly submissions. These are account allowances, rather than a separate allocation for each generated app, and they may have changed, so confirm them on Jotform's current pricing page. Paid plans provide more submissions.

Before you launch, compare expected usage with the current account limits. An onboarding app serving a few clients has different requirements from an event app collecting hundreds of registrations.

The most common mistakes are unclear requests, oversized first versions, and sharing before testing. Each can be reduced by defining a smaller task and reviewing the result.

This workflow is best for defined business tasks. Projects requiring extensive custom backend behavior need a separate assessment, such as the developer route described above.

Before sharing, confirm that the app serves its intended audience and that its content, behavior, and settings have been reviewed. A short launch checklist helps keep the review consistent.

Here are some frequently asked questions about the output, account requirements, and practical limits of the Jotform App Builder plugin.

Yes. With the plugin installed and authorized, plain-language requests can generate and revise an app in ChatGPT.

The plugin can create a Jotform App combining pages, forms, and resources. Follow-up prompts can refine its structure, content, and design.

Customer portals, internal tools, event apps, business websites, directories, onboarding hubs, and booking or ordering apps are some of the suitable starting points.

A Jotform App is the finished app created and maintained through Jotform. A custom GPT configures a conversational assistant, while a plugin or application made with OpenAI developer tools involves a separate development workflow with its own build, authentication, and publishing steps.

Understanding how to create an app in ChatGPT means keeping the roles clear: ChatGPT supports the conversation, the plugin creates and revises the Jotform App, and the Jotform editor handles closer editing and configuration.

Start with a focused task, then review the content, permissions, and behavior. Test the complete experience with sample information before sharing it with the intended audience.

#10732. 6 Best Coding Apps for Beginners to Learn Programming

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Learning to code can feel overwhelming at first. There are different programming languages, development tools, and concepts to understand, and choosing where to begin is not always easy. Coding apps can make the process more approachable by breaking lessons into smaller steps and giving beginners a place to practice what they learn.

Whether you want to build websites, develop software, or explore programming as a new skill, the right app can help you get started. Some platforms focus on interactive lessons, while others offer coding exercises, guided projects, or access to a full development environment.

Here are six coding apps worth considering if you're learning programming for the first time.

Mimo is a beginner-friendly coding app designed to help users learn programming through short lessons and interactive exercises. Rather than starting with lengthy explanations, it introduces concepts in manageable steps, making it easier to fit learning into a busy schedule.

The app covers several topics, including Python, JavaScript, HTML, CSS, and SQL. This makes it useful for beginners who want to explore general programming or focus on web development. As learners progress, they can work through structured learning paths and practice applying concepts through coding exercises and projects.

Mimo is particularly suitable for people who prefer a guided learning experience instead of figuring out what to study next on their own. Its mobile-first approach also makes it convenient to practice during breaks or while traveling.

A free starting option is available, although access to some courses and features requires a paid plan.

Best for: Beginners who want structured lessons and practical coding exercises.

SoloLearn is a coding education platform that helps beginners explore programming languages through short lessons, quizzes, and hands-on practice. It is a useful starting point for people who want to understand programming fundamentals before committing to a specific development path.

The platform offers courses in languages and technologies such as Python, JavaScript, Java, C++, SQL, HTML, and CSS. Its bite-sized lessons make complicated topics easier to approach, while interactive exercises let learners test their understanding as they progress.

Another useful feature is its learning community, where users can explore other people's code, participate in discussions, and learn from different approaches to programming problems.

SoloLearn works well for people who are still deciding which language to learn. However, completing lessons alone is not enough to develop strong programming skills. Beginners should also practice writing their own programs and building small projects.

The platform offers free learning options alongside paid features.

Best for: Beginners who want to explore multiple programming languages and learn through short, interactive lessons.

Codecademy Go is the mobile companion to Codecademy's online learning platform. It is designed to help learners reinforce programming concepts and review material while away from their computers.

Codecademy's broader learning platform offers courses covering web development, Python, JavaScript, SQL, and other technical subjects. The mobile app can be useful for reviewing concepts, practicing through supported activities, and maintaining a consistent learning routine between longer study sessions.

For example, someone taking a JavaScript course on a computer can use the mobile experience to revisit concepts and strengthen their understanding before returning to practical exercises.

It is important to distinguish between learning on a mobile device and developing software on one. Codecademy Go is primarily a companion for learning and review rather than a complete replacement for a desktop coding environment.

Some learning content is available without a subscription, while access to additional courses and features depends on the current plan.

Best for: Learners who want to review programming concepts alongside a structured online course.

Programming Hub is an educational app for people who want to explore programming and related technical subjects in one place. It brings together lessons, examples, and exercises designed to introduce users to different areas of software development.

Its learning material covers languages such as Python, Java, C++, and JavaScript, along with web development and other computing topics. This variety can be helpful for beginners who want to compare different languages before choosing one to study in depth.

The app's lesson-based approach makes it possible to explore a topic without immediately installing a development environment or configuring complicated software. Code examples and practice activities can also help learners connect theoretical concepts with practical applications.

However, beginners should avoid jumping between too many languages at once. Starting with one language, understanding its fundamentals, and applying those skills to a small project will generally provide a stronger foundation.

Programming Hub offers free and paid options, with access depending on the selected plan.

Best for: Beginners who want an introduction to several programming languages and computing topics.

Enki takes a slightly different approach to learning programming by emphasizing regular practice and the development of technical skills over time. Its lessons and exercises are designed to help users strengthen their understanding through consistent, manageable learning sessions.

Depending on the learning content available, users can explore topics such as Python, SQL, JavaScript, data analysis, and other areas of technology. This makes the app relevant to both aspiring developers and people who want to improve their technical knowledge for work.

One advantage of short, recurring practice sessions is that they encourage learners to revisit concepts instead of studying them once and moving on. Regular revision can help reinforce programming syntax, terminology, and problem-solving techniques.

Enki may be especially useful for people who already have some exposure to coding and want to maintain a learning habit. Complete beginners should check whether the available lessons match their chosen language and current skill level before starting.

The app offers different access options, so check its current listing for details about free and paid features.

Best for: Learners who prefer regular practice and gradual improvement in technical skills.

Replit is a browser-based development platform that allows users to write, run, and test code without setting up a traditional development environment on their computer. It is particularly useful for beginners who want to move beyond lessons and start creating their own programs.

Users can work with languages and technologies such as Python, JavaScript, HTML, and CSS, depending on the project and environment. Instead of only answering quiz questions, learners can experiment with code, identify errors, and see how their changes affect a working application.

For example, a beginner learning Python could create a simple calculator, while someone studying web development could build a basic personal website. Starting with small projects helps learners understand how individual programming concepts work together.

Replit also provides AI-assisted development features, which can help users explore ideas and understand code. However, beginners should review generated code and learn why it works rather than relying on automated suggestions without understanding them.

Replit offers a free starting option, with additional capabilities available through paid plans. Features and usage limits may vary.

Best for: Beginners who want to practice writing code and experiment with real projects in a development environment.

The best coding app depends on your learning goals, preferred learning style, and current experience. Before choosing one, consider what you want to accomplish.

Choose a language first. If you want to learn general programming, Python is a reasonable starting point. If your goal is to build websites, HTML, CSS, and JavaScript are more directly relevant.

Consider how you learn. Apps such as Mimo and SoloLearn emphasize short lessons and interactive practice. Codecademy Go is useful for reviewing course material, while Replit provides an environment for experimenting with code.

Look for practical exercises. Understanding a lesson is only the beginning. Choose resources that encourage you to write code, solve problems, and apply concepts in small projects.

Check the cost. Several coding apps provide free introductory content, but some courses and advanced features may require subscriptions. Review the current pricing before committing to a paid plan.

Move beyond the app. As your skills improve, practice using a computer, read technical documentation, and create projects of your own. These activities help you develop the problem-solving skills required for real programming work.

Coding apps provide an accessible way to begin learning programming without needing extensive technical knowledge or complicated software installations. They can help you understand basic concepts, practice regularly, and discover which areas of development interest you most.

Mimo and SoloLearn are worth exploring if you prefer guided lessons, while Codecademy Go can complement a structured course. Programming Hub and Enki offer alternative approaches to learning and practice, and Replit provides a way to experiment with working code.

You do not need to master every programming language or use several apps simultaneously. Start with one resource, focus on a single learning goal, and gradually apply what you learn to practical projects. Consistent practice and curiosity will take you further than simply completing lessons.

#10728. AI, DevOps and the Cost of Hosting: From Idea to Production for $0

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

An AI coding agent rebuilt my photo-collage app as a distributed system and deployed it to a free server. The hard part turned out to be hardware: one free ARM CPU, 538 collages created during load testing, zero failed jobs, and $0 a month.

Like most developers, I have never been short of product ideas. Hosting them has always been the problem. I have tried shared hosting with SmartWeb, Azure free credits, Google Compute Engine’s free VM instances, SmartWeb’s virtual private servers, and Heroku dynos. None of them left me satisfied. The worst experience was a $200 charge for an AI address API on Google Cloud—for an application that never earned me a single kobo.

I am not a cheapskate, but I learned quickly that one of the easiest ways to kill a potentially good idea is to get the finances wrong. An application has to stay online until it reaches a critical mass of users, and hosting an untested, unverified product on expensive hardware can be wasteful.

Agentic AI coding has changed how quickly I can move from idea to production. Over about five weeks of intermittent coding—amounting to roughly three days of full work with Claude—my photo-stitching app went from an Angular 8 project that would not even build to a distributed system running in production.

Along the way, Claude upgraded the front end from Angular 8 to Angular 22 and fixed the build on Node 24. It set up RabbitMQ with Docker Compose so the message broker configures itself on startup, and added real-time notifications over SignalR with a progress spinner. It fixed a crash loop caused by an unreadable iPhone photo. It redesigned the system so any server can process any job, with switchable database and file storage, direct browser-to-storage uploads, and photos resized in the browser before upload. It added reliability work that a team might debate for a sprint: a dead-letter queue, confirmed publishing, idempotent workers, recovery of stuck jobs, and automatic clean-up. It fixed the stitching so photos keep their proportions and borders are even. It wrote 41 automated tests and scrubbed leaked passwords and personal photos from the Git history.

Coding, QA, DevOps, and production support used to be separate phases, often handled by separate people. Here they happened in one conversation. When code becomes this cheap to produce, the bottleneck moves. For me, it moved to hardware.

To check what the AI builds, you have to run it locally. A distributed app means running the whole system locally: a web app, a background worker, a message broker, a database, and storage emulators. Because Claude adds features quickly, and each one can add another container, it is easy to exhaust a laptop’s RAM and CPU.

My setup made this lesson concrete. I work on a MacBook Air, with Visual Studio in a Windows virtual machine. Docker cannot run inside that VM on Apple Silicon, so Claude ran Docker on the Mac and drove it from Windows over SSH. Later, my mobile connection started dropping large packets and broke uploads from the VM, so Claude moved the load testing onto the server itself. The software problems took minutes. The hardware and network problems took longer.

Then comes hosting. Heroku looked like the easy route, but it has had no free dynos since 2022. The cheapest option is an Eco dyno at about $5 a month, plus about $5 for Postgres. That is affordable, but running it for an idea that has no users yet did not seem prudent.

Claude suggested Oracle Cloud’s Always Free tier: an ARM server with up to 4 CPUs and 24 GB of RAM, free with no end date. I created a small instance with 1 CPU and about 6 GB. Claude wrote a production Docker Compose file and a one-command setup script: Caddy as the gateway, the web app, the stitching worker, RabbitMQ, and PostgreSQL, with data that survives restarts. My repository is private, so rather than put a GitHub token on a public server, it copied the code over SSH and deployed it. The last blocker was mine to fix: opening ports 80 and 443 in Oracle’s network firewall. Then the site was live.

I did not want to guess, so I asked Claude to load-test the live server. Simulated users ran the full flow: upload two photos, wait for the real-time notification, and download the stitched collage.

The server handled 64 people creating collages at the same moment without a single failed job: 538 collages, zero errors. Its ceiling was about 62 collages a minute—one per second. Beyond that, users queue. With 4 people creating at once, 95% of collages arrived within about five seconds; at 64, each person waited about a minute. If each active user makes a collage every couple of minutes, one CPU comfortably serves around 85 people at once, and a few hundred if they use it less often.

The test also showed what runs out first, and it is not the CPU. Each collage stores about 5 MB, mostly the PNG output, so at full speed the disk would fill in under two hours. Switching to JPEG and shortening retention fixes that, and when traffic grows, three more free CPUs are waiting. That is the value of measuring before paying: I know what to change, and when.

Oracle isn’t being charitable. Servers need applications to run—ideally ones that go viral—and the free tier is a play for loyalty. Oracle wants successful projects to stay and grow into its paid services instead of moving off the platform once they reach critical mass. It can also reclaim free instances that sit idle.

I think the bet is smart, and it points to a bigger trend. AI-generated code still needs energy to run and hardware to host it, even when far fewer people work on each project. As more people turn ideas into working software, demand for compute will keep growing, and I expect hosting and data centres to become a very profitable business.

For now, the combination works: Claude orchestrates the coding, QA, and DevOps, and Oracle’s free server keeps an idea online long enough to find out whether it deserves to grow.
